Rosie Huntington-Whiteley Goes Completely Backless in a Sculptural Gucci Dress

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

She made a statement at the 2026 LACMA Gala

At the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala, Rosie Huntington-Whiteley was all business in the front, party in the back. Showing up to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art’s annual black-tie event—where everyone from philanthropists to actors to musicians to athletes came together for a glamorous evening of fundraising—the model was seen wearing a sleek black dress with a surprise.

Courtesy of Gucci, the evening’s sponsor, the piece appeared simple and modest at first glance—with a front defined by a sculptural, exaggerated high neckline and a slim-fit long-sleeve, floor-length silhouette. When Huntington-Whiteley turned, however, the dress was revealed to be (almost) entirely open, with a singular piece of silver hardware creating separation between a low scoop back and a high leg slit.

And, if that wasn’t enough, the whole garment was lined with dark-green sequins, which shimmered subtly in the light. She matched the shade to her diamond-and emerald earrings, which were on full display thanks to her slicked-back ponytail. She slipped into pointed-toe black pumps and stopped for photos in front of Chris Burden’s famed public art installation, Urban Light, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening.

The Art+Film Gala celebrated its 15th year tonight, supporting the museum’s ongoing efforts to expand its film programming. It honored the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic work exploring the complexity of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for a musical performance, and, like every year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

Digital Approval Workflow: How to Audit Your Current Setup

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A digital approval workflow allows businesses to streamline documentation processes and ensures the right people are approving requests. But an approval workflow doesn’t necessarily operate efficiently in practice.

By carrying out an audit, organizations will have a better understanding of how their approval process is operating. This will allow you to identify any inefficiencies in your approval process.

What is a Digital Approval Workflow Audit? It is a structured process to review your digital approval processes. It can help identify whether you are making sure requests are being approved correctly and efficiently.

It can also help you identify any redundant processes, delays, missing documentation and lack of ownership. A typical digital approval workflow audit will look at:

Whether your approval workflow is structured, Who is responsible for approving requests, Whether requests are being approved efficiently, Are there any documents in place to document the process, Is there an audit trail, Are sensitive documents only being accessed by authorized users and can managers see how approvals are happening.

The aim is not to look for errors in your process but understand how your process is operating and whether it is meeting your operational requirements.

Why Should Businesses Audit Their Digital Approval Workflow? Regular audits will help ensure your approval process is running efficiently.

One of the most common issues with an approval workflow is requests getting stuck in limbo as nobody has approved them. By carrying out an audit you can identify where delays are happening and whether certain stages are causing unnecessary delays.

For example, purchase requests may need to go through 3 managers when they just require 1 or 2.

Every approval should have an owner. If you don’t have clear owners it can cause confusion amongst staff members as they won’t know who should be approving a document.

By carrying out an audit you can identify whether every stage has an owner and whether decisions are being documented.

You need to ensure you have reliable documentation in place for any important decisions. An approval audit trail should include all the relevant details such as the request, supporting documents, who approved, time stamps, comments and what the outcome was.

Having this information will make it easier to review previous decisions and identify any discrepancies. Structured approval processes will make it more useful for you than emails.

4.Reduce Manual Processes By carrying out an audit you can identify any repetitive processes. For example, staff may be manually sending requests, sending reminders and updating spreadsheets.

This will help you identify which processes you can automate.

Different departments may have different approval standards. This can lead to inconsistencies in decisions.

By reviewing your approval workflows you can ensure they are operating using standard processes. Whilst still allowing specific requirements for different types of requests.

A successful audit should cover the whole approval process.

For example, a purchase approval may follow this process:

Request has been made by an employee -> Reviewed by the department manager -> Checked by the Finance team for budget-> Final approval by Senior Manager -> Decision has been received by employee.

Make a note of all the different stages, including any manual processes and how information is being communicated. This will help you to identify if there is a structured process in place.

Ensure everyone has an owner for each approval.

Having approval tied to roles will help stop any disruption to your workflow if employees change roles.

Ensure you have rules in place for where requests are going.

Are approvals in the correct order for the type of document? Some processes may require sequential approvals whilst others may be able to have multiple people reviewing at once.

Are there any extra approvals being triggered based on criteria? For example, purchase value, type of document or risk.

Having a clear structure will help reduce any unnecessary approvals.

Review how long requests are taking to go through each approval stage.

Look for any patterns such as: Requests remaining pending for a few days, Repeated follow-up from employees, Approval stages always causing delays, Requests being sent back due to missing information, Unnecessary review steps.

Useful metrics to track include average approval time, number of requests in limbo, rejection rate and the number of times requests have been revised.

This will help you identify if delays are caused by the approval workflow, unclear ownership or incomplete submissions.

A good digital approval workflow should have a clear audit trail.

Name of requester and submission date, Details of request, Supporting documents, Names/roles of approvers, Approval/rejection, Time stamps, Comments, Reassignments, escalations and status, Final approval.

Ensure users can view previous versions of documents. Your audit trail should give enough information so you can see what happened during the approval process.

Approval workflows often include confidential financial, operational and business information. Therefore you should review your security.

Check If: Employees can only see relevant information, Approvals are in line with organizational authority, Sensitive documents are secure, Previous employees/inactive accounts have the correct restrictions, Important actions are being documented, Backup processes are in place.

You should also ensure your processes are in line with any internal policies and legal/regulatory requirements.

Notifications will help your approvers know when they need to do something. However, if they aren’t managed well it can cause unnecessary communication.

During your audit check whether notifications being sent at the right point and pending requests are being escalated.

For example, if a manager isn’t responding within the set time frame the request can be sent to an authorized backup approver.

See if your notification settings useful, deadlines are realistic and escalation rules are clearly communicated.

Those who use the approval process regularly can provide useful feedback on any issues.

Ask your team members whether the process is easy to understand, or requests are often delayed, whether they experience having issues accessing documents?

Once you have completed your audit you should split your findings into 3 categories:

Critical issues – Security issues, unauthorized approvals, missing documentation or important compliance requirements.

Operational issues – Delays, lack of ownership, continual requests for information and unnecessary approvals.

Improvement Opportunities – Better reporting, automated reminders, simpler forms and improved integration.

Ensure each issue has an owner and set a realistic timeframe for resolving. Prioritize any issues based on how much they could impact your organization.

Carrying out a digital approval workflow audit will help you understand whether your approval process is efficient, secure and has good ownership. By reviewing your approval stages, ownership, documentation, audit trail and performance you can identify any issues.

Using a audit checklist will help make the process easier to manage. You should also review real examples of approvals, ask for staff feedback and create an improvement plan.

Most importantly you should regularly carry out audits. This will help ensure you have consistent approvals, reduce delays and create adaptable workflows.

Auto-detecting file types taught me guesses need an escape hatch

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 8 • Originally published at imapp.blogspot.com

I shipped a single endpoint that takes any document and returns Markdown, and the first week was mostly about the bytes before the conversion. PDF, DOCX, PPTX, XLSX, EPUB, HTML — none of them announce themselves reliably. Magic numbers lie, extensions lie more, and a PDF renamed .txt is still a PDF.

The lesson I keep returning to: when you auto-detect, you are making a guess on behalf of the caller, so the guess has to be inspectable. I added a detected_type field to the response and a way to force the parser when the guess is wrong. That turned silent corruption into a boring error the agent can correct on the next call. Most "bad output" reports weren't converter bugs; they were wrong sniffing.

The second lesson is that one door only works if the door tells you what went through it. Eight endpoints felt honest, but callers really wanted "give me text" plus metadata: source type, page/slide/sheet count, truncation flags, warnings. Agents chain these tools; they need the receipt, not just the result.

If you're shipping in this space, build the failure path first. The happy path is a demo. The unhappy path is the product.

Apache APISIX Admin Exposure: The Gap Between a 9180 Listener and a Key-Protected Control Plane

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apache APISIX uses its Admin API to change routes, upstreams, consumers, and other gateway configuration. The official documentation shows the Admin API on port 9180 and uses an X-API-KEY header for administrative requests. That makes the control plane an attractive asset to inventory. A public listener is not proof that the API is unauthenticated, and a missing key in a scan is not proof that the service is vulnerable. The useful question is how many assets can be associated with APISIX, and which of those associations are strong enough to inspect.

On November 8, 2026, ZoomEye count-only queries for this assessment used sub_type=all, page 1, and page size 1. The exact application fingerprint app="Apache APISIX" returned 0. The title query title="Apache APISIX" returned 11,114. A listener-oriented query, port="9180" && service="http", returned 3. These are matched asset counts at one collection time. They are not counts of vulnerable gateways, and the page-size setting limits returned records without changing the matched total.

The gap between the title and application counts is the first finding. The title result is much larger and may include documentation sites, dashboards, login pages, reverse proxies, or pages that mention Apache APISIX without exposing its Admin API. The application result is precise within the ZoomEye fingerprint vocabulary, but a zero means the selected fingerprint did not match this corpus at that moment. The listener query is narrower but still only identifies an HTTP service on a commonly documented port.

That distinction matters during triage. An asset with the product title but no Admin API listener should not be treated as a control-plane exposure without another signal. An asset on 9180 should not be assumed to be APISIX merely because the vendor documents that port. The matching application fingerprint, certificate data, response headers, and a safe non-destructive request can help classify the asset. Missing evidence should move the asset to "unknown", not to "confirmed vulnerable".

The authenticated control plane needs a separate ownership model. APISIX documentation describes an Admin key and access-control settings such as allow_admin. Teams should restrict the listener to management networks, require TLS and a strong key, rotate keys on a planned schedule, and review whether the key is present in configuration management or logs. An internet-facing Admin API should have a documented exception and monitoring for configuration changes.

ZoomEye is useful here as an inventory source, not as a vulnerability oracle. The title query can find candidate deployments. The port query can find exposed listeners that need ownership confirmation. The application query can support a clean asset match where the fingerprint is available. A team should preserve the query, collection time, type scope, count, and the reason each asset was selected. If a query returns an error, it should remain an error rather than being recorded as zero.

The next step is to join external observations with internal evidence. Compare the candidate address with the gateway inventory, certificate inventory, DNS ownership, cloud account, and change tickets. Use an authenticated, authorized request from a controlled network to check the service version and configuration. Confirm whether the Admin API is bound to localhost, a management interface, or a public address. Confirm whether the key is enforced before any route-changing endpoint is reachable.

A count alone cannot answer those operational questions. It can tell the team where to look and how the public fingerprint differs from the internal inventory. APISIX's Admin API is powerful because it changes traffic behavior. That makes the exposure review an identity and network-control problem, not a search-result problem.

AI Ships Fast, but Focus Drifts

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One incident still sticks with me. Our application services have to update a batch of data in shared memory atomically. That memory is managed centrally by the system: cross-process locks, memory ordering, and version switching are all wrapped inside the .so library, and callers reach it through a single shared header.

At the time, though, the library offered no interface for "atomic bulk update". The AI's approach was about as blunt as it gets: grab the physical address through mmap, overwrite field by field according to its own reading of the layout, and casually invent a flag on the side to serve as a "currently being modified" lock.

In a single process it ran fine, and the tests passed. The trouble is that what it bypassed was not an ordinary function but an entire system contract: the cross-process lock no longer held, the memory-ordering guarantees were gone, and any other process reading that memory could hit half-written garbage at any moment. Worse, the bypass leaves no trace. When the library later needs auditing or a memory layout change, those "ghost call sites" poking straight at physical addresses will be impossible to track down.

The AI wasn't out to sabotage anything. It simply took the path of least resistance when nothing provided the capability it needed. And that path happened to water down the contract "atomicity is guaranteed by the layer below" into "every call site makes up its own guarantee".

The longer I use it, the more of these moments I collect: locally flawless, globally full of holes. They tend to show up in three ways:

First, abstraction boundaries collapse. Like the mmap case above. In ordinary development, when a library lacks an interface, the caller and the provider sit down and agree on where the boundary is and how to define it. The AI makes no such judgement; wherever the rules don't reach, it fills in the gap itself.

Second, nothing accumulates in the foundation layer. An experienced engineer will pull common logic into a shared class along the way. AI tends to solve in place: this feature needs it, so add a method to the current file; the next feature needs it too, so write it again in another file. Nobody breaks the foundation layer — it simply never gets built.

Third, layered conventions get ignored. In any moderately complex system, technical constraints come in layers. Take the industrial control and data acquisition system we build: the platform layer is strict C++11 with exceptions disabled, while the application layer above it may use C++14. That means when creating a smart pointer, the platform layer has to write std::unique_ptr p(new T(args...)) by the book, while the application layer can use std::make_unique. To the AI, though, the whole repository is flat. The gcc in the dev environment is new enough that make_unique slips into the platform layer and still compiles; the problem only surfaces at integration testing.

A person might remember that C++11 rule. What a person can't do is spend an afternoon driving the AI through a dozen-plus sessions and five features in quick succession, and still remember which cell of the layering diagram the current session is sitting in.

Pull these observations together and they point at one thing: when AI coding spans sessions, the contracts between features lose their carrier.

System software is inevitably assembled from hundreds or thousands of features. You can't opt out of contracts; you can only choose whether to write them down, or to keep them in your head and carry them in prompts. Experience shows the head doesn't retain them, and stuffing them into prompts is a long-shot bet — miss one layer of context and you have a production incident.

Someone might ask: aren't SDD (spec-driven development) and Code Plan First (plan-first mode) meant to do exactly this?

No. The reach of both patterns stops at "get the current task right". You spell out the interface rules in session 1's spec, and nothing guarantees the AI still remembers them in session 50. You can, of course, copy "must go through the library's single entry point" and "no make_unique in the platform layer" into every feature's detailed design, over and over. That is unreasonable to expect of anyone, and it will inevitably miss something. A problem that sits at the boundary of a pattern can't be patched by "using the tool better".

Traditional documentation is even less help. It gets split by role into isolated islands of requirements, architecture, and testing, in wildly varied formats — I have seen companies whose requirements docs are PowerPoint decks because that makes them easier to present upward. A few rounds of code iteration later, the documents contradict each other. You can't ask an AI to consult a pile of stale dead artifacts like that.

So the problem is not AI's comprehension or its output quality — those have actually exceeded my expectations. To put it plainly: AI is not the problem. Our way of organizing is. Our current model of project management cannot keep up with this new productivity.

To hold the contracts between features in system software, we need an independent carrier that outlives a session — one that both a person and an AI can read precisely, and whose structure makes explicit which rule governs which layer.

So along what dimension should this long-lived document be organized, if we don't want to repeat the same mistake?

Jurassic Park Meets Fallout In New Steam Game You Can Try For Free

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There is, frustratingly, a severe lack of games like Fallout. The series has been burdened with a reluctant ownership of the retrofuturism aesthetic and the first-person shooter RPG subgenre, something it, unfortunately, has utilized to poor effect on account of their having been only two Fallout titles released in the past decade. Beyond the likes of Atomfall and The Outer Worlds, there have been too few Fallout-likes to satiate the endless appetite of fans smitten with its alluring blend of 60s Americana and the apocalypse.

Fortunately, while the trickle is painfully slow, there are a handful of games like Fallout slipping through the cracks in the hopes of usurping Bethesda's legendary RPG or, at the very least, offering fans an alternative. One such title is a brand-new first-person game that blends Fallout with Jurassic Park to surprisingly good effect, delivering a truly unique experience that prospective fans can try for free on Steam. If you've been longing for a game like Fallout, this may just be the title for you.

In lieu of a Fallout 5 release date announcement or really any news regarding a new Bethesda release, fans are understandably turning to indies and AA experiences. Fortunately, Wilderdark, the aforementioned Jurassic Park meets Fallout game, aims to appease those fans, so long as they don't mind a little bit of Dino Crisis-esque horror thrown in for good measure. Wilderdark is a first-person title focused on investigating an island inhabited by dinosaurs, all while desperately attempting to survive.

Much like how Atomfall veered more toward enhancing Fallout's focus on survival, Wilderdark leans closer to being a survival horror experience than a full-blown RPG. Indeed, in many ways, it appears to have beaten the upcoming survival horror-focused Jurassic Park game to the punch, thus appeasing the fanbases of two criminally underserved franchises. Wilderdark, naturally, also leans further into the horror elements of Fallout, leveraging its developer, Team Junkfish's expertise in the survival horror genre to deliver some genuine scares.

In Wilderdark, you explore assorted areas in search of evidence and samples, all while avoiding the blighted dinosaurs roaming around. You'll need to take photos using your trusty camera, gather resources in order to survive, and pay close attention to your surroundings as you piece together the mystery of the island and its prehistoric inhabitants. Team Junkfish's last survival horror title, Monstrum, proved it understands the genre, and Wilderdark looks set to further its ambitions of delivering cinematic horrors.

If Wilderdark sounds even remotely interesting, you can try it for free via its official Steam store page. The available demo offers a small slice of the overall experience, ostensibly serving as a proof of concept for the full game. Some users have reported that the demo is riddled with bugs and crashes frequently, which is something to take into account should you encounter any issues. The full game doesn't have a release date yet, but one can hope that this Fallout-like title ends up releasing soon, if only so that fans have something to enjoy while awaiting Fallout 5.

Why coding agent memory files go stale - and what teams should do instead

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 8 • Originally published at getlockstep.dev

A coding agent reads a memory file that says the checkout migration is blocked on database approval.

The approval landed last week. The migration shipped. The task closed. Nobody removed the note.

The agent now plans around a blocker that no longer exists.

This failure is easy to misdiagnose as weak retrieval. The agent retrieved the exact memory the team gave it. The problem is lifecycle: temporary state survived without an owner, status, or expiry, so old context still looked current.

Memory files go stale when they mix information with different lifetimes:

Do not solve stale memory by adding more memory.

A file often starts with a few useful notes:

When every line looks identical, the agent cannot tell which one is current or authoritative.

A deployment, owner, ticket, or customer configuration moved on. A copied note did not.

A blocker or next step survived after the branch merged.

Search retrieves the idea without the verdict.

The old record remains useful history but no longer governs current work.

A rule written for one provider or service gets applied everywhere.

Root, nested, personal, and team rules disagree. The agent may pick one arbitrarily.

The maintenance strategy depends on which form you have.

A tool may store several categories, but the records still need distinct fields and lifecycle rules.

Not every note needs every field. Significant team decisions do. Temporary handoffs need task and status. Live facts need a verifiable source and observation time.

A timestamp alone is weak. The newest note may still be an unapproved proposal. Status and ownership establish meaning.

When a decision changes, keep the old record but mark it superseded and link the replacement.

The new record should name what it replaces. This lets an agent follow an old search result to the current answer and preserves the reason the system used to behave differently.

Deleting history removes useful context. Leaving old history unmarked creates false current context.

Do not persist facts that the source can answer better:

Persist the instruction to check the source. Retrieve the fact when it becomes action-driving.

This reduces duplicates and keeps freshness tied to the system that owns the state.

When the task completes, remove .handoffs/current.md or mark it closed and move it to history. Add cleanup to the definition of done so it does not depend on memory.

A simple CI or review check can flag a current handoff that points to a closed task or has not changed for a chosen period.

Cursor recommends focused rules and referencing source files rather than copying their content. Claude Code recommends concise CLAUDE.md files and warns that conflicting instructions may be followed arbitrarily.

Instead of pasting a decision into three rules:

Then maintain one record. A pointer can still become stale, but it is easier to check than several duplicated explanations.

Sources: Cursor Rules and Claude Code memory

Automation should surface candidates. Owners still decide whether the context is wrong.

Then change one fact, close one task, and supersede one decision. Ask again.

The setup passes when the agent retrieves the current answer, follows replacement links, and does not treat closed task state as active.

History accumulates, but nothing tells retrieval what wins.

Old decisions may remain valid for years. Lifecycle and ownership matter more than age alone.

This removes history and makes old code harder to understand. Keep replacement links.

Similarity cannot establish approval or current status.

Duplicates drift at different times. Query the owning source.

For the full map of memory layers and how they fail, start with the AI coding agent memory guide. For the wider market, see the best tools for AI coding agent context and best MCP memory servers for teams. For exact storage boundaries, use CLAUDE.md vs agent memory.

For managed choices, compare decision log tools for product and engineering. For repository decision lifecycle, see ADRs for AI coding agents.

Lockstep gives team decisions the lifecycle fields generic memory files often miss: accepted choice, rationale, rejected options, owner, scope, status, and explicit replacement.

Agents retrieve the current record through MCP. Product and engineering can review or supersede the same decision outside the coding session. Temporary task state stays in handoffs, changing facts stay in live systems, and instruction files point to the current decision instead of copying it.

The goal is not a memory file that never changes. It is a context system that makes change visible before stale information changes the code.

Elliptic Traces $124M in Bitcoin Swaps From Binance Hack Funds

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: A November 6 update from blockchain intelligence firm Elliptic says roughly $124 million of assets stolen in the October Binance breach had been converted into Bitcoin, including funds originally held as XRP and SOL. Onchain tracing can reveal movement, but it cannot by itself identify every person controlling those wallets or prove that all the converted funds have been cashed out.

The laundering trail from the huge October cryptocurrency theft attributed by investigators to North Korean-linked actors continues to evolve. In a November 6 update to its technical analysis, Elliptic said the value of stolen assets converted into Bitcoin had reached approximately $124 million.

The headline figure is striking, but the more useful story concerns how the funds moved, what remains traceable and why conversion into Bitcoin is not the same as spending money through the conventional financial system.

Elliptic’s investigation describes activity involving tens of thousands of wallets, multiple networks and an assortment of services. By its November 6 checkpoint, analysts had identified about 1,650 BTC held in new addresses following conversion from other stolen assets. The firm said roughly $50 million worth of XRP had begun moving largely towards Bitcoin, while stolen Solana tokens had also been swapped, bridged or mixed.

The firm presented this as an ongoing, rapidly changing investigation. An estimated figure tied to a timestamp should not be reported as a permanent total, and wallet clusters can change as further transactions occur.

Cross-chain movement complicates tracing because the deposit and payout sides of a transfer may occur on different networks. It can also make the trail harder to follow when funds pass through pools, aggregators and intermediaries. However, the public nature of blockchain transactions and the ability to connect wallet activity through known services can still help investigators reconstruct substantial portions of the flow.

Elliptic reported that approximately $2.85 billion of the original stolen assets had been frozen and about $3.11 billion remained in addresses linked to the theft at its latest checkpoint. The firm cautioned that only a negligible portion appeared to have been cashed out. Such estimates may change with later tracing, and asset values vary with market prices.

This matters because a criminal actor may hold large amounts of Bitcoin or Ether for some time before attempting to move value through brokers or exchanges. Public addresses can show coins being shuffled, but they generally cannot prove the real-world identity of each subsequent controller or the final ownership arrangement.

The distinction is especially relevant to compliance services. Screening against attributed addresses can alert businesses to potential sanctions or illicit-funds exposure, even when the funds have crossed networks. Yet address blacklists and automated risk scoring are not perfect substitutes for investigation and human judgement.

The evolving theft occurred alongside a different security incident affecting Chainalysis, another blockchain-analysis provider. The two incidents should not be conflated. Chainalysis described its own systems and credentials issue as unrelated to the Binance theft, while Elliptic’s work here concerns tracing funds from that separate exchange incident.

For operators, the lesson connects to the custody, counterparty and incident-response themes covered in NewsBTC’s reporting on digital-asset infrastructure restrictions. Protecting users involves controls over withdrawals, counterparties and incident communications — not simply watching one chain’s transaction explorer.

The next meaningful updates will concern verified freezes, attributed movements to identifiable services and evidence of actual cash-out. Elliptic’s data show the stolen funds are still moving through complex paths. They do not yet tell a complete story about who ultimately receives them or how much can be recovered.

9 Near-Perfect Dragon Age Replacements For Fans of Fantasy RPGs

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Messy, morally gray RPGs wherever you look

BioWare’s Dragon Age is a fantasy RPG series about warring factions, world-ending threats, and, yes, the occasional dragon – but that’s the first-glance take. Get into one Dragon Age game (start with Origins!) and you’ll see that the true heart of this series is the people you drag into hell with you.

The choices you can make in Dragon Age are messy. Your companions get legitimately attached to you — or not; your dating life can complicate your mission; and innocuous decisions you make in hour one can affect the sweeping political options available to you in the series’ biggest moments. Whether you seek more of that exact mix of party banter, branching loyalty quests, and a world that remembers you, or you’re intrigued by the same bones in a different skin — sci-fi crew instead of fantasy party, perhaps — here are 9 games to add to your queue.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: The cast of well-written characters and intricate combat system (along with Larian Studios’ whimsical charm)

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Although Divinity: Original Sin and its sequel are set 1,000 years apart, in both, you’re protecting your fantasy world, Rivellon, from a magical energy (Source) and monsters emanating from the series’ overarching Void. But, luckily, you’re not tackling that alone. Each game gives you the opportunity to gather a party of intrepid explorers to save the world, but, more importantly, bicker constantly as you do.

This dynamic within Divinity: Original Sin and especially Divinity: Original Sin 2 makes the characters the true reason for replay after replay. Sure, you’re solving a murder mystery in Divinity: Original Sin and escaping imprisonment, then surviving the wilds in Divinity: Original Sin 2 — but those are details. Wait until you meet Sebille. You’ll either be best friends or unable to stand each other immediately.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s a 2002 game that helped form today’s RPG genre of real-feeling companions and branching questlines

Where to play: Windows PC, PS4, PS5, Switch, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Before Dragon Age, there was Neverwinter Nights — a fellow BioWare title that paved the way straight to Dragon Age. At the start of the game, a fatal plague called the Wailing Death (chill; normal) is striking the city of Neverwinter. You’re in quarantine, but you still need to graduate from the Neverwinter Academy and figure out how to help Lady Aribeth create a cure for the plague. People are panicking. There are riots to navigate. Everything’s absolute chaos behind the city’s closed walls.

Since Neverwinter Nights is nearly a 25-year-old-game, some parts of it are harder to love than others, but it’s worth a play for Dragon Age players seeking a nostalgic title.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got similar-feeling companion depth and combat primed for a party tactician

Where to play: Windows PC, PS5, Xbox Series X, Switch

In Warhammer 40,000: Rogue Trader by Owlcat Games, you’re exploring uncharted space…specifically, the Koronus Expanse. But while the setting differs from mostly land-based Dragon Age, the problems your characters face are similar. Here, you’re navigating a succession crisis and working to claim your place within the Von Valancius family. Much like Dragon Age’s Blight and Darkspawn, Rogue Trader has its share of existential corruption for you to weather (here, it’s Chaos and Warp mutations) while you also have to deal with the possibility of much more immediate betrayal.

Dragon Age players will recognize Rogue Trader’s starting class and character background system – will you be a Warrior who was a military officer, or a priest-turned-Operative? – but the turn-based combat will be a change of pace.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: Everywhere you turn, there are fiendishly difficult narrative choices. Buckle up

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Switch, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Welcome to Baldur’s Gate 2 — surprise, you’re trapped in an underground laboratory. You’re Gorion’s Ward, a Bhaalspawn (e.g., kid of the murder god Bhaal). There’s a devilish wizard, Jon Irenicus, who’s torturing you – ahem, conducting tests to study your essence. You need to escape, rescue your also-trapped childhood friend Imoen, and then rest and recover…as long as nothing happens to interfere with that plan, of course.

In Baldur’s Gate 2, as in the Dragon Age series, you’re learning about the world around you and figuring out who you can trust or recruit to help you accomplish a goal that’s both very personal and potentially world-ending. But here, there are vampires.

Baldur’s Gate 2 is another BioWare title from before the company moved away from D&D, so the two games also share developer DNA. (As a bonus, if you’re a Dragon Age person and you’ve not played Baldur’s Gate 3…please do.)

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got the same BioWare choice-and-companion architecture. Just swap your sword for an assault rifle

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Mass Effect and Dragon Age are both BioWare RPG franchises with relationship building, storytelling, and party- or squad-based tactics. In each, your choices shape the narrative. In each, you can use save data from one game to influence the next. In each, you have a base — a camp or a spaceship — where you can regroup with your companions.

But that’s where the similarities end. In Mass Effect, you start as newcomer Commander Shepard aboard an advanced starship. You’re working to investigate a beacon on a human colony world…until things may or may not very suddenly take a turn for the worse (brace for attack!). But at least you’re fighting alongside friends. Getting to hang with the sullen, sarcastic Garrus Vakarian might be one of the greatest joys in the series.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got party-based tactics and large-scale kingdom management.

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

If the political games you could play within Dragon Age: Origins were the draw for you, Pathfinder: Kingmaker has that in spades. With even more management systems layered on top. Here, you start the game as a group of adventurers gathered by one Lady Jamandi Alordi. You’re going to the Stolen Lands to bring back the head of the Stag Lord. If you do, you get to found your own barony. If not…well, let’s not contemplate that alternative, shall we?

While this game isn’t just routine kingdom management – there are mysterious fey threats and an ancient curse getting in the way of your day-to-day – steering Pathfinder: Kingmaker’s plot via being a decent lord to your barony is a big focus in this game. But, if you’re looking for explicitly similar structure to feel anchored here, Dragon Age: Origins players rejoice: the combat is real-time-with-pause, which should feel just like home.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s got grudge-holding factions, possibly-loyal companions, within a Colonial-era setting replete with political intrigue.

Where to play: Windows PC, PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

Less polish, smaller budget, rougher edges, but a lot of the same charm – GreedFall feels like Dragon Age in that, here, you’ve got real-time combat, a world where your choices matter, and the ability to deeply bond (or not) with your companions. In GreedFall, though, instead of battling the Blight, you’re working to save the old continent from a plague, the Malichor. Your character is a Legate tasked with traveling to an island untouched by the plague to discover the cure, but you’re not the only one with that idea. As multiple colonial factions – including the religious nation Thélème and the fond-of-science Bridge Alliance – fight over the island, your job is simple: Protect the island, search for the plague’s cure, and fend off political disaster. That does sound simple, right?

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: It’s a crunchier, more tactical game: Think Dragon Age run through a true Dungeons & Dragons ruleset.

Where to play: PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

As Solasta: Crown of the Magister begins, your party of four is called upon to investigate the Badlands – a land infested with monsters, but you’re prepared for that, right? You do find the titular Crown of the Magister along the way, an artifact that might just be able to help you save your world.

Dragon Age players who loved the series’ focus on deep character writing may find Solasta a bit lacking. Solasta’s focus is not on story. Instead, you’re managing turn-based combat, organizing inventories, and figuring out how to use your class abilities to their greatest effect.

To the “less of a story” point, though, Solasta’s sequel is in development. It’s theorized that the team’s leading harder into story this time around, so stay tuned.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

Why you’ll like this if you like Dragon Age: While this game doesn’t have traditional team management – you’ve got pawns, not true parties – it does have the same dark fantasy tone and ingrained class build system.

Where to play: PS4, PS5, Windows PC, Xbox Series X

A gigantic dragon has just attacked the fishing village of Cassardis. When you step up to defend, the dragon rips out your heart, eats it, and leaves you for dead. Oh no! Game over. Except it’s not: You wake up as an Arisen. You’re now bound to the dragon’s fate. You need to find it and get your heart back. Have fun!

Dragon’s Dogma is very different mechanically from Dragon Age, but it sets a similar scene and feels in-genre anyway. You won’t find deep companion writing here, but what you will find are dragons. Did you play Dragon Age for dragons? Extremely understandable. You’ll love Dragon’s Dogma. We cannot overstate how many dragons there are.

With Dragon Age, BioWare helped build a genre of games where stories and companions come to life on a grand scale – but there’s still the quintessential battle RPG focus on skill progression and party tactics. Interested in saving a fantasy continent from the plague? More interested in building your own barony? Regardless, there’s something here to play. And replay, with different choices. And replay, as an evil overlord. And then replay again, just for fun.

BioWare's swan song now leaves a grand legacy

8 DC Heroes Who Would Be Better Green Lanterns Than Fans Expect

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Green Lantern Ring is one of the universe’s most powerful weapons, and the Guardians choose their champions based on their ability to overcome great fear. While iconic Green Lanterns such as Hal Jordan and John Stewart are worthy wielders of the ring, the allure of wondering how other heroes would fare is far too great.

The DC universe is full of heroes with the right psychological profiles and traits to make fantastic Green Lanterns. Willpower isn’t in short supply in DC, but some heroes are so fearless, mentally resilient, and willful that they could be even better Green Lanterns than fans realise right away.

This article spotlights eight heroes who would make better Green Lanterns than fans might expect. From street-level heroes to some of DC’s heaviest hitters, these unsung figures have exactly the traits needed to master the ring. They are already formidable, but a Green Lantern ring would unlock an entirely new layer of their potential.

Batman uses fear to keep criminals in check, which makes him a strong candidate for a Yellow Lantern ring, but fans may also not expect the Dark Knight to become a powerful Green Lantern. The animated series The Batman (2004) shows how effective he would be with the ring.

In the series, after Sinestro attacks him, Hal Jordan sends his ring to Gotham City to find Batman. Although the ring initially falls into the Penguin’s hands, it eventually reaches Batman. With the ring on a low charge, Batman fights and defeats Sinestro, showing he is adept at using the Green Lantern ring.

Overall, Batman is one of DC’s best strategists, a master of all kinds of combat, and an extremely disciplined crime fighter. With a lantern ring, Batman can put his strategic mind to good use, turning the ring into the ultimate tactical weapon. When it comes to sheer willpower, very few heroes can match the Caped Crusader.

The Green Lantern Corps has never lacked power, but it has often lacked wisdom at the top. The Guardians have repeatedly made poor decisions with potentially devastating consequences, including creating the Manhunters. As a result, Diana of Themyscira would not only be a powerful addition to the Corps, but also a great leader.

Diana has commanded Amazon armies, anchored the Justice League through its darkest crises, and served as an ambassador between gods and mortals. She is as wise as she is powerful, and her skills would be best used leading the Green Lantern Corps as a member.

Beyond her outstanding leadership and strategic mind, she is also a fierce warrior and a compassionate hero. Diana is a proud Amazon warrior, and bravery and willpower abound on Themyscira. With a Green Lantern ring, she transforms from a hero who can hold her own against Superman into one of the most powerful Green Lanterns.

John Constantine is nothing if not creative. His unprecedented ability to think on his feet and battle powerful otherworldly threats makes him a top-tier Green Lantern. Constantine’s entire career is defined by staring down literal demons, hellish overlords, and cosmic horrors while armed with nothing but raw nerve. It’s not that he’s fearless; he’s just immensely courageous.

His occult expertise could become twice as dangerous when combined with a Lantern ring. His mind could generate ethereal binding runes, hard-light sigils, and metaphysical cages. He could also create devastating constructs of powerful demons and otherworldly beings, crafting intricate esoteric constructs designed specifically to exploit his opponent’s psychological and mystical weaknesses.

Giving a Speedster a Green Lantern ring basically makes them unstoppable. That is even more true when the Speedster is the Flash. Whether it is Barry Allen or Wally West, the Speed Force demands incredible mental fortitude to wield properly. The Flash sees most things in slow motion, giving him plenty of time to create ideal constructs.

Pairing the Green Lantern ring’s ability to operate at the speed of thought with the Flash’s faster-than-light speed thinking means a Flash with a ring could generate millions of unique hard-light constructs instantly. The Flash would have ample time to create constructs that defy the imagination before his enemies can process what is happening.

A Green Lantern ring is limited only by its wielder’s imagination, which makes Jaime Reyes a perfect candidate. The Blue Beetle scarab works much like the Green Lantern ring: it reads intent and creates weapons the user can imagine. Giving Jaime a ring would not only be a power-up, but it’s right in his wheelhouse.

Furthermore, Jaime’s life is defined by constantly resisting the scarab’s lethal programming and standing his ground against cosmic threats. Overcoming the fear of losing control of his body shows immense willpower, but his real advantage is his bond with extraterrestrial technology, which would give his constructs an unimaginable edge.

Instead of generating static shields or basic blades, his mind would produce dynamic, evolving weaponry that mirrors Reach tech. His constructs would instantly adapt to target vulnerabilities, morphing mid-battle, making him a deadly combination of human creativity and machine logic.

When fans think of Raven, they immediately picture shadow magic and the dark, demonic influence of her father, Trigon. However, her entire life has been a constant exercise in mental discipline and emotional control. Holding back absolute evil from consuming her soul takes astonishing willpower, proving her mind is stronger than most people imagine.

Her unique psychological training would give her constructs an unprecedented edge. Coupled with her mastery of magic, Raven would make a uniquely terrifying Green Lantern, able to turn nightmares into reality through powerful constructs. Her intense focus, sharpened by years of keeping inner turmoil at bay, would produce constructs of rare density and absolute stability.

Mister Miracle’s whole existence is defined by resisting the overwhelming, soul-crushing terror of Darkseid and the pits of Apokolips. With impeccable escape artistry and unshakable willpower, he did the unthinkable and escaped Darkseid’s prison and grasp. For Scott Free, surrendering to fear was never an option; his unbreakable determination to break every chain makes him a natural fit for willpower.

Moreover, a Green Lantern ring lets the user turn raw imagination into hard-light constructs, and Scott’s mind is uniquely suited to construct creation. Having mastered every escape technique in the cosmos, Mister Miracle has a creative mind that would lend itself to devastating, intricate constructs.

While Superman is a Symbol of Hope in the DC universe, he also has incredible willpower and is an ideal candidate for a Green Lantern ring. The core requirement of a Green Lantern is not just possessing willpower, but the ability to overcome great fear, and Superman has this in abundance.

The Man of Steel may have unimaginable power; he routinely faces beings strong enough to kill him, such as Darkseid and Doomsday. He never backs away from a confrontation when the world is in danger. Moreover, with a super-genius-level intellect and super-speed cognition, he can create highly detailed and creative constructs.

Furthermore, willpower powers the ring and determines how solid the constructs are. Superman’s willpower and mental discipline, sharpened through years of controlling his immense powers, would make him an incredibly powerful Green Lantern. Earth Green Lanterns usually protect Sector 2814, but Superman already does that and more every day.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

‘The Invisible Guest’ Remake Director Danial Rifki on Suharto-Era Fear and Why His New Thriller Is About ‘How to Fight Back’

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Indonesian director Danial Rifki’s remake of Spanish thriller “The Invisible Guest,” which he says was completed in 2023 before exclusivity clauses with HBO held up its release, finally reached the big screen at Jakarta World Cinema.

It is one of three Rifki films at the festival.

The Falcon Pictures production, which reworks Oriol Paulo’s 2016 hit “Contratiempo” for an Indonesian setting, stars Reza Rahadian, Mawar de Jongh and Arifin Putra. It debuted on HBO Max on Aug. 7. Related Stories Murder-House Impostor Thriller 'The Stand-In Family' Wins Mirror Fiction Award at Taiwan Creative Content Fest 'Jimmy' Review: Cornball James Stewart Biopic Gets the Manner, but Not the Man

Rifki tells Variety that the matter has only recently been resolved.

He also has two new features at the festival: romantic comedy “Tua Tua Keladi” and thriller “Sandal Jepit No. 10.”

Set among the older residents of a small village, “Tua Tua Keladi” follows the chaos that breaks out when a chemical leak into the village water supply stirs up desire. Unspoken feelings, long-held confessions and passion come out overnight.

“I was thinking, if they have young energy to live the second life, [it is] the second chance to love,” Rifki says.

He wanted the film to challenge a deep-rooted assumption that older people no longer live with energy. For all the awkwardness of its premise, the film keeps away from eroticism, even with its younger characters.

In the digital era, Rifki says, people can record their sexual behavior openly – “and sometimes that kind of freaks me out because I have a daughter. I’m concerned if my daughter [is exposed to] wild media.”

“So with the young characters, I try to approach them with something pure, not physical,” he says.

“Sandal Jepit No. 10” is a confined-space thriller about the brutal police interrogation of Akbar, a grieving father detained on suspicion of murder.

“Indonesians have trauma in politics. Life back in the days of Orde Baru – the New Order in the Suharto era – for around 32 years, we could not criticize the government,” Rifki says. “Now we have the Reformation Era, but it might be happening underground. On social media, if you criticize the government, they will shadowban [your comment].”

“We are the low class of society with this kind of fear that, if we do something, someone with power will oppress us. So the movie is about how to fight back,” he says.

“If you have social media, netizens are here to help you out. People power now is turning [to] a digital era so we can gain that power again. I’m still sure that [there are] many good people,” he adds.

Rifki says the film grew out of his concern over abuse of power in Indonesia today.

“[When I was] a film student, I really wanted to try how to speak in rom-com, thriller, drama, and other genres. [I’m] an evolving and growing filmmaker still in the making,” he says.

The backbone of all his work, he says, is human moral conflict, whatever the genre. He is also keen to try action and horror.

His next film, “Before Thirty,” is set for release in January 2027. Rifki calls it a “survival drama” about a 29-year-old man whose life is failing.

“In Indonesia, people always ask: what is your achievement before 30? Young people are punished by that value that you have to be successful before the age of 30,” Rifki says.

The film, he says, is his “commentary on that issue.”

Alex Consani Defies Gravity in a Twisted Gucci Gown at the LACMA Gala

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Alex Consani is no stranger to a gala, and tonight, she brought her signature glamour to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art’s annual fundraiser. The event—the LACMA Art+Film Gala—celebrated its 15th edition this evening, supporting its ongoing efforts to expand its film programming. And like every year, a whole lineup of familiar faces, from models to actors to philanthropists to musicians to athletes, showed up to participate.

Joining fellow attendees such as Cardi B, Nicole Kidman, and Paris Hilton, Consani arrived in a theatrical, gravity-defying gown. Courtesy of Gucci, the gala’s sponsor, the look twisted around her body, with an exaggerated, asymmetrical strapless neckline that extended far past her right shoulder. Its long, voluminous pleated skirt was finished with a zig-zag hem that gave the whole thing a witchy touch, and she wore it with pointed-toe heels.

Before heading inside, the model stopped for photos by Chris Burden’s Urban Light—a public art installation-turned-red carpet composed of oversize street lamps.

The 2026 gala, co-hosted by Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow for the 15th year, recognized the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic work that explores the complexity of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. For live music, Blackpink’s Rosé joined a historic roster of past musical guests, including Stevie Wonder, Florence and the Machine, Elton John, and Charli XCX.

Consani first attended LACMA’s event last year, wearing another Gucci masterpiece. This one was a sheer, shimmering champagne gown, with a plunging halter neckline and ultra-high slit that exposed her entire left leg. The floor-length piece was covered in sequins, and she paired it with matching nude heels.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: Is anyone stocking up supplies?

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →



It boils down to what your goals are and what you consider reasonable measures to guard against the possibilities. I think there's a lot of bang for the buck to be had from having several days of food and water on hand, but that's more of a side effect of having a family of four, a house in the suburbs, and a Costco membership. I also have vehicles that can get us a state away, in an environment where extended road closures of any magnitude are extremely unlikely. Of course, there's more mileage to be had from being a part of a community--having a group of friends or family willing to step up in the case of emergency--rather than a bunker mentality of trying to go it alone. I'm fortunate to have those resources, along with financial resources, that make surviving a disaster more likely than others without those resources. Again, my goal isn't to ride out a hurricane or earthquake or whatever in my home with no change in living standards, it's to ensure the safety of my family. Katrina, probably the worst disaster to his the U.S. in my lifetime, killed 1,300 people--essentially zero of which were wealthy suburbanites who starved to death or died of a waterborne illness.

And we're allowed to have different risk assessments--if you're interested, you can find all manner of forums across the internet (spanning various political outlooks) who think a breakdown of civil order is imminent. I think that's silly, and even if it's not, impracticable to guard against. But those who take it seriously often reach the logical conclusion that they should already be living like they plan to after it happens. Those people will be prepared for that scenario, basically no one else will be no matter how many buckets of food dust they keep in their basement. I note that you apparently think that a deadly airborne pathogen is a likely enough possibility that it's worth taking steps to prepare against, yet you're apparently unprotected against that deadly pathogen that could already be circulating or could start to circulate later this afternoon. You're drawing a line, like everyone else that isn't already living in their apocalypse bunker. I'm suggesting that unless you derive significant value from fantasizing about the breakdown of society and being the ant rather than the grasshopper, the financially prudent move is to put yourself in a position to survive long enough for services to be restored or to be able to relocate the types of events that have happened to your area or areas like it rather than wasting a bunch of money preparing for, say, an EMP or widespread civil unrest, both of which I consider (1) extremely remote possibilities, and (2) things that would take significantly larger investments in time and money to even begin to adequately prepare for.
You completely didn't answer my question: Where are some good places to buy water if the water supply becomes unreliable for a lengthy period of time? I'm assuming you've personally seen store shelves completely bare before e.g. a big predicted hurricane or blizzard. The water aisle is picked 100% clean. As are other staples like bread, milk, eggs, etc. I was replying to the part of your post where you said that water was available for purchase in lengthy water outages, and that hasn't been true multiple times per year where there is a warning of imminent snow (in the South people freak out) or imminent hurricane. Was just trying to ask you since I genuinely don't know of any places someone can purchase water in a water emergency with empty water shelves in the stores.
In any major American city, often are those store shelves bare AND methods of egress to areas with water (either via pipe or store shelves) inaccessible for more than a few days? With very few exceptions, hurricanes and earthquakes don't kill people through extended lack of access to clean water. Maybe you disagree, but you also disagree with me about whether it's worthwhile to prepare to hang tarps and create positive pressure environments to keep out widespread airborne pathogens. I submit that's due to a fundamental difference in the way we see risk, and that's fine, but for those who assess risk more like me than you, I think this is useful information.

Since Jodie Sweetin and Andrea Barber launched their Full House rewatch podcast, How Rude, Tanneritos!, plenty of BTS sitcom tea has been spilled. From the fact that producers had a preferred Olsen twin to work with to the anger on set when Candace Cameron Bure cut her hair without warning, there's been so much to learn about the TGIF show. And, on the latest podcast episode, it was revealed that one actor had “too big a pack” to be wearing biker shorts for a scene with Kimmy Gibbler, and this sounds so cringey.

In the season 7 episode of “Love on the Rocks,” DJ Tanner debates whether or not to break up with her boyfriend, Steve Hale. When she goes mountain climbing with Kimmy and her boyfriend, Keanu, she gains a new perspective on her life and relationship. While discussing the episode, in a recent episode of How Rude, Tanneritos!, Kimmy actress Andrea Barber exposed a rather revealing detail about her on-screen boyfriend that cropped up amid filming:

This poor actor, Keanu. So originally, he wasn’t wearing his jacket and it was just his shirt and his biker shorts. But, the bike shorts were a little tight. And a little too revealing.

Yes, poor actor! When you rewatch the episode, the biker shorts Keanu was wearing really did look tight, almost like spandex. And for a family-friendly show, it makes sense to cover that up. Like Sweetin humorously said, that’s just too much information for families at home to see.

It’s common for wardrobe changes to happen when an actor on a family-friendly sitcom is showing a little growth down there. That happened to Jaleel White when he was asked to hide his bulge in Family Matters. In the case of Brian Evans, who played Keanu, Barber explained that the wardrobe department had other ideas to hide that area:

They got a note. Costumes got a note from Video Village. And so, they had to quickly find this jacket. A long jacket to cover.

Well, I’ve got to say, the long jacket worked. You can’t see anything. Hey, you do what you have to do to keep the Full House episode G-rated.

Anyone curious as to how Brian Evans felt about the embarrassing wardrobe change should know he told EW that he “didn’t know what was happening.” Not only that, but he also revealed the priceless reaction his mother had when one of the crew members told him he had “too big of a pack" for a teenager on this show”:

I remember seeing my mother’s face sitting at the studio audience just beet red, laughing.

This certainly isn't the kind of moment I would've expected to hear regarding the long-binge-watchable show, but it's also a funny piece of information. Thank goodness the costume department of the ABC sitcom was right on top of it. Otherwise, I could imagine the producers receiving some not-so-flattering letters from parents.

It’ll be hard not to think about this little (or not-so-little) tidbit of information when watching this Full House episode moving forward. Still, the detail shouldn't be too distracting for audiences given how the jacket comes into play. Fans can watch “Love on the Rocks” and more episodes of the show by streaming them with either a Hulu subscription or a Disney+ subscription.

Just your average South Floridian cinephile who believes the pen is mightier than the sword.

I Love Obsession Breakout Inde Navarrette’s Relatable Take On How Twilight Has Been Helpful Amid Her Whirlwind Year

I didn't think she could get any cooler, and yet...

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

There’s a lot of love about Season 1 of Prime Video’s popular hockey romance Off Campus. The book-to-screen adaptation has incredible chemistry, love triangles, shirtless hockey guys, and even a live performance from indie pop-rock star Remi Wolf. I’ve been spamming her music ever since the show came out, including her new album Mud. Her raspy belt is incredibly unique, and I only just found out that it actually got her pretty far on American Idol.

I’ve actually been a fan of Remi Wolf for a few years now, so I was pleasantly surprised when I started watching Off Campus, and she popped up as herself on stage in Season 1, Episode 1, which is available to stream with an Amazon Prime subscription. However, I had no idea this wasn’t her first TV appearance. Wolf was actually first introduced to audiences on Season 13 of American Idol, and recently reminisced with People about her experience on the iconic singing competition:

I got pretty decently far, and it was a cool experience to see how the entertainment industry works because I had no experience whatsoever in that world.

Wolf, who at the time was 17 years old, made it decently far in the competition after auditioning “on a whim.” She wowed judges Harry Connick Jr., Jennifer Lopez, and Keith Urban to make it to Hollywood Week before getting cut from the competition. However, the “Bottle” singer said she was grateful her time on Idol ended there:

I was such a baby, and I’m so glad that I was able to carve my own path as a baby growing into an adult rather than having that early influence on me by executives and stuff. I feel like my frontal cortex was not developed enough to handle it.

One thing I love about Remi Wolf’s music is that it’s her own unique sound, and I love that she has built her career off living completely true to herself, and that Island Records, the label she is signed to, has allowed and encouraged her to do that. I have to wonder if teenage Remi Wolf in 2014 could have predicted that her run on Idol, and eventual exit, would have led to a cameo on one of Prime Video’s most acclaimed shows of 2026.

Wolf first gained fame from her hit single “Photo I.D.” in 2021, and has since released three studio albums. Her performance in Off Campus features some of her biggest songs off her second studio album Big Ideas, including "Soup", "Toro", and "Alone In Miami."

Her latest album Mud was released on October 9 of this year, with the 30-year-old describing the project on her Instagram as “my favorite, my healer, my family, my precious, my blood, sweat, and tears.” It’s clearly her most vulnerable project to date, prompted by her decision a year and a half ago to become sober.

I would love to see Remi Wolf return to Off Campus in Season 2, which has officially been confirmed to drop on Prime April 28, 2027. The “Sexy Villain” singer said on the Zach Sang Show that she would be down to return, but that she has no idea where they would work her into the story, and I have to agree. However, I would love to hear some of her new songs off Mud included in the soundtrack when Season 2 premieres.

Quand la vis d'Archimède devient un moteur moléculaire : mon plongée dans NexoPeek

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

J’ai lu l’article de ScienceDaily le 30 octobre 2026 en sirotant un café froid dans mon bureau de thèse. Un moteur moléculaire bidirectionnel, inspiré de la vis d’Archimède, actionné par l’électricité, capable d’augmenter la conductivité d’un facteur 100. Une machine vieille de deux mille ans, réinventée à l’échelle du nanomètre. La phrase a fait tilt : « Et si je pouvais vraiment voir cette vis tourner, sentir la torsion entre deux atomes, mesurer la distance angström qui sépare le rotor du stator ? »

Depuis des mois, je bataillais avec des fichiers PDB sur un écran 24 pouces, à tourner des modèles en boule, à zoomer, à cliquer, à exporter des images pour mes articles. La structure de ce moteur (code PDB 8XYZ, déposée la veille) était là, quelque part dans RCSB, mais l’explorer restait une gymnastique de souris. J’ai pensé à mon casque Meta Quest qui dormait dans le tiroir, acheté pour tester des prototypes de visualisation VR. J’ai ouvert l’application NexoPeek, développée par Seven Sportz, et j’ai lancé le mode « Professional PDB Viewer ».

La recherche intelligente a résolu le nom du gène en un clin d’œil : grâce à la résolution de synonymes UniProt, le moteur a été récupéré depuis RCSB PDB, UniProtKB, AlphaFold DB, SWISS-MODEL et PDBe en une seule requête. Pas de copier‑coller d’identifiants, pas de téléchargement manuel. Le modèle s’est affiché en mode « cartoon » avec le preset Mol* « Publication », prêt pour une capture d’écran de qualité article.

J’ai basculé en « Room View ». Le moteur moléculaire a flotté au centre de mon bureau, les parois de la pièce restant visibles grâce au passthrough. J’ai pu marcher autour, tendre la main, attraper le rotor virtuel avec mes doigts (le suivi de main natif du Quest fonctionne sans contrôleur). La sensation d’échelle humaine — le rotor fait environ 5 nm de diamètre, mais ici il occupe la place d’un ballon de basket — change radicalement la perception de la géométrie.

Curieux, j’ai ouvert l’outil « AI Pocket Prediction ». Le moteur propose deux moteurs : le cloud‑based PrankWeb/P2Rank et un moteur hors‑ligne intégré. J’ai lancé le premier ; en quelques secondes, une série de poches druggables s’est illuminée en rouge translucide sur la surface du stator. L’interface affiche le score de chaque poche, le volume, les résidus impliqués. J’ai noté la poche 3, proche du site de liaison du ligand expérimental, et j’ai lancé le « Ligand Inspector » pour y charger le composé co‑cristallisé (récupéré via PubChem en un clic). Le réseau de liaisons hydrogène s’est dessiné en traits pointillés, et j’ai pu vérifier la distance exacte entre l’oxygène du ligand et l’azote d’une lysine clé : 2,8 Å, mesuré avec le « Distance Calculator » en pointant simplement les deux atomes.

Ensuite, j’ai voulu comparer ce moteur à une hélicase connue pour voir si le mécanisme de torsion était conservé. J’ai utilisé l’alignement structurel basé sur Foldseek : j’ai chargé la structure de la hélicase (PDB 5ABC) depuis AlphaFold DB, lancé la superposition, et obtenu un RMSD de 1,9 Å sur le cœur du domaine. Le rapport d’alignement, généré automatiquement, m’a donné les résidus équivalents et m’a permis d’émettre l’hypothèse que le même principe de vis pourrait être exploité pour concevoir un inhibiteur allostérique.

Tout cela s’est passé en moins d’une heure. J’ai pu exporter des rendus haute résolution (cartoon, surface, B‑factor heat) directement depuis les six presets Mol* sans quitter le casque. J’ai même enregistré une courte vidéo en mixed reality pour la présenter à mon directeur de thèse lors de notre réunion hebdomadaire : le moteur flottant au-dessus de la table de conférence, les poches druggables pulsant, les distances angström s’affichant en temps réel. Il a souri, a dit « C’est exactement ce qu’il nous faut pour le prochain grant ».

Ce qui m’a le plus marqué, c’est la fluidité entre le mode éducatif et le mode recherche. Les douze visites guidées — « Biotechnology Basics », « Foundations of Life », « Pathways & Interactions », « Medicines in Action », « Genetic Technologies », « Green Biotech » — sont narrées, basées sur des structures réelles du PDB, et m’ont permis de revisiter les bases (hémoglobine, glycolyse, CRISPR‑Cas9) avant de plonger dans mon analyse pointue. Le passage de l’un à l’autre se fait sans rechargement, grâce à une architecture modulaire qui sépare le moteur de rendu (Mol* via WebXR) du backend de recherche (API REST vers RCSB, UniProt, AlphaFold, SWISS-MODEL, PDBe) et des services d’analyse (PrankWeb, Foldseek, PubChem).

En tant que développeur, j’apprécie que l’application supporte l’ingestion de fichiers locaux (.pdb, .cif, .mol2, .gro, .xyz, .dx, .xtc, .py) et permette d’y superposer des champs électrostatiques, des définitions de liaisons personnalisées ou des volumes de densité électronique. Cela ouvre la voie à des pipelines personnalisés : on peut générer une carte de potentiel avec APBS, l’exporter en .dx, la charger dans NexoPeek et la visualiser en mixed reality lors d’une réunion de labo.

Bien sûr, NexoPeek reste un outil de visualisation et d’aide à l’analyse, pas un dispositif médical. Les prédictions de poches et les alignements structuraux sont des indicateurs, pas des preuves. La documentation le rappelle clairement : vérifiez toujours les résultats auprès de la littérature primaire et de méthodes validées avant de prendre des décisions expérimentales.

Ce soir, après avoir éteint le casque, j’ai regardé le moteur moléculaire tourner encore dans mon esprit. La vis d’Archimède, vieille de deux millénaires, tourne maintenant à l’échelle du nanomètre sous mes doigts virtuels. NexoPeek m’a offert ce pont entre l’histoire de la mécanique et la biologie structurale moderne, sans quitter mon bureau.

Si vous voulez explorer vous‑même des structures à échelle humaine, tester la prédiction de poches par IA, aligner des domaines avec Foldseek ou simplement suivre une visite guidée narrée sur CRISPR, le casque Quest et NexoPeek sont là.

Essayez‑le ici : https://www.meta.com/experiences/nexopeek/1453797551140347/
